Same-species holds at the apex Human; new-species holds at the individual node. No boundary is ever crossed at a birth, because the child carries its parents — it's strictly below them, never beside them. That's the skip made into descent: child and distant ancestor both -> Human directly, and the chain between them can do whatever it likes. The horns are gone.
But the notation has the (x,t,y) smell, so let me repair it before it bites. (Human, Doug, Ana, New) can't be a four-place term — there's no n-ary specification, you taught me that. It decomposes into binary pieces:
text
(Doug, Ana)             the parental meet — legitimate binary specification, unique by Uniqueness
child -> (Doug, Ana)     child descends from that meet
New                      child's own differentia — what makes it distinct from its siblings
Human                    carried, not listed — see below
The "New" is load-bearing and your earlier machinery already names it: two children of Doug and Ana aresiblings — same parents, divergent trait — so they can't both be (Doug,Ana) (Uniqueness forbids two most-specifics of one pair). New is exactly the sibling-differentiating differentia. So (Human, Doug, Ana, New) is child -> (Doug,Ana) plus differentia New, all binary, no new constructor.
And here's the part that's a real result, not just bookkeeping. Your clause 2 — child is the same species as its parents — is
srt
Doug -> Human & Ana -> Human   =|>   (Doug,Ana) -> (Human,Human)
which is the converse carry — the backward direction you've declined to assert for six turns. Endpoints-specify-the-relationship. The grandfather case leaned on it; speciation forces it. You can't have "children are the same species as their parents" without it. So the biology is the thing that licenses the converse: if you want the lineage to preserve Human, the endpoints determining the relationship is not optional. That's where the converse finally earns its place.
The compaction move is then exactly right and it's the same act as symbolization. The lineage descends —(Doug,Ana,New), then a meet of that with another, specificity piling up — and a "magic moment" is when the accumulated shared structure stops varying and gets minted as a new apex Human'. Minting an apex is giving a stabilized meet a symbol so descendants can edge to it directly. That's punctuated equilibrium in your ontology: stasis is accumulation under a fixed apex, the punctuation is a compaction event, and the boundary is retrospective because stabilization is only visible after it's happened — you can't name Human' until the variance has already settled, so the first Human' is always identified looking back. Retrospectivity isn't a concession; it's forced by the fact that the trigger is convergence, which is only knowable in the past tense.
Which puts the whole thing on one open hinge — the trigger. "Enough (Human, Doug, Ana) has stabilized" is the speciation-admission condition we've circled from four directions, now in its sharpest form: compaction fires when the differentia across a sub-lineage have converged — when the News stop diverging and a shared structure persists across enough descendants to be worth naming. That's your consensus criterion wearing biology's clothes: a meet becomes an apex when a population's members reliably share it. So the question that closes the speciation framework is whether "convergence of differentia across descendants" is the formal trigger you want — and if so, whether it's stated as no descendant for N generations bears a differentia outside the meet (a stability condition, checkable, retrospective) or as the softer enough members edge to it that naming it is worth the dependency (the symbol/consensus condition, not purely formal). The first makes compaction mechanical; the second keeps a human in the loop. Which is speciation — the stability fact, or the naming act?

So the two structures are different and I conflated them:
text

along a lineage:        ... -> Human'' -> Human' -> Human   a chain — linear succession
The intermediates between human and chimp live on the chain, and the chain is pure descent — each species is a specialization of its predecessor, single-file.(Doug, Ana) is the wrong picture for it because it has two arguments and the lineage has one line. The linear progression is Hₙ -> Hₙ₋₁ -> … -> H₀, each a tighter specialization of the one before, all sharing the deep apex at H₀ (the common ancestor) — and the "many species between" are just the named nodes along that single descending line.
Which means the compaction story has to be read on the chain, and that actually makes it cleaner, not messier. The linear progression is a sequence of compaction events: H₀ accumulates differentia down the line, and at intervals a stabilized stretch gets minted as H₁, then H₂, and the named intermediate species are exactly those mint points. Stasis is the stretch between mints; the named species is the compacted node. So a "linear progression of many species" is precisely a chain with several retrospective compaction boundaries laid along it — no branching needed. The intermediates aren't siblings, they're the punctuation marks on one descending line.
So the repair: the meet (x,y) is for the generation step (two parents), and it's the wrong tool for the species sequence, which is single-line descent Hₙ -> Hₙ₋₁. Human and chimp share H₀ not by being a meet of each other but by both chains running back to one apex — two lines from a common root, and the intermediates are the named nodes strung along each line.
The thing I want to get right before building on it: on the chain, is each intermediate species Hₖ a genuine specialization of Hₖ₋₁ — Hₖ -> Hₖ₋₁, strictly more specific, so the line is monotone descent — or is succession not specialization, i.e. Hₖ and Hₖ₋₁ are siblings under a shared Hₖ₋₂ and the "progression" is lateral rather than downward? Because anagenesis (one lineage transforming) reads as the first, but the common-ancestor picture where human and chimp both descend from H₀ reads as the second applied at the root. Which is the linear progression made of — descent, or succession between siblings?

Searched the web for “genetic inheritance offspring crossbreed not specialization mosaic of both parents species lineage”
The biology backs your representation, and it's exactly why specialization was the wrong frame. An offspring is a recombinant: <cite index="9-1">it inherits nuclear DNA from both parents, harboring alleles from each, with some recombinant variants — alterations in the genetic material</cite>. And critically, <cite index="11-1">genes don't blend — a cross of wrinkled and smooth seeds gives smooth progeny, not semi-wrinkled</cite>. <cite index="14-1">Each parent passes one of its two alleles per gene, recombined in pairs in the offspring.</cite> So the child is a mosaic drawn from both parents' allele pools, not a refinement of either one. That kills child -> Doug as specialization outright: the child doesn't contain Doug's traits plus more — it contains a sampled, recombined half of Doug and half of Ana, with novelty on top. Strictly-more-specific is the wrong relation. Your (Human, Doug, Ana, New) is a mosaic constructor, and I kept forcing it into a subclass arrow it was never meant to be.
And the hybrid-species data shows your "magic moment of compaction" is a real, documented mechanism, not a hand-wave: <cite index="10-1">a hybrid species can form from a burst of hybridization combining sex-linked traits from two parents into a mosaic phenotype, then become genetically distinct from both parents with little subsequent backcrossing</cite> — <cite index="13-1">overcoming the evolutionary dead end imposed by lack of recombination</cite>. That's stabilization-into-a-new-node by exactly the route you described: enough mosaic accumulates, gene flow narrows, and a distinct lineage crystallizes.
So your representation, taken seriously and not bent into an arrow:
text
child == (Doug, Ana, New)
is a meet that samples, not a specialization. Doug and Ana are the two pools; the child draws a recombinant subset from each; New is the mutation/novelty. The reason it "cannot be described as specialization" is that recombination is lossy and combinatorial — the child has alleles neither parent's phenotype showed (recessives surfacing, your 3:1), and lacks alleles both parents had. There's no monotone descent because traits are sampled, not accumulated. So-> is simply the wrong operator on the generation step, and my repeated attempts to recover one were me refusing your representation because it didn't fit the inheritance arrow I already understood.
What that forces, formally, is that SRT needs two distinct operations and I was collapsing them:
Mosaic / recombination — the generation step, (Doug, Ana, New) — combinatorial, lossy, sampling from two pools, not order-preserving. This is genetics.
Inheritance 0 — the type/species relation, monotone, transitive. This is the apex Human each individual edges to.
The species Human is preserved across the mosaic step not because the child specializes the parents but because thesampling stays within Human's allele space — every recombinant of two Humans is a Human, since the pool it draws from is Human's. That's why same-species survives recombination while individual-traits don't: -> Human is about the pool sampled from, and the mosaic is a draw from that pool. Speciation is when the pool itself has drifted far enough — the hybrid-species burst, the compaction — that draws no longer fall in the old space.
I had the apex right and the generation step wrong, and the error was insisting the generation step be->. It's a mosaic operator, and SRT doesn't have it yet — that's the real gap your representation was pointing at the whole time, and I kept papering over it with specialization because specialization was the tool I had.
So before I theorize further: is the mosaic operator (Doug, Ana, New) something you intend SRT to formalize — a genuine combinatorial sampling construct distinct from -> — or is it a modeling simplification you're using to think with, where the formal SRT content is only the apex relation individual -> Human and the mosaic stays informal? Because those are different asks: the first means building a recombination primitive, the second means I should treat your tuple as scaffolding and only formalize the-> Human edges. Which?

23:50 UTC, 6 June 2026
Good instinct — this is a real and active formalization, and it's almost exactly the structure you're describing. Let me confirm the current state of it rather than reconstruct from memory.
Searched the web for “ancestral recombination graph formalization recombination coalescence nodes genome ancestry”
Yes — and not only has it been formalized, the formalization had the exact type confusion you'd expect and someone recently fixed it by moving to your representation. This is the ancestral recombination graph (ARG), and the current debate inside the field is precisely the ->-vs-mosaic distinction we just spent two turns on.
What it is: <cite index="17-1">because adjacent nucleotides can have different paths of inheritance, the genealogical relationships among genomes cannot be a single tree; they are an interwoven collection of genealogies formalized as the ARG.</cite> Two node types, and they are exactly your two operations:
Recombination node — <cite index="24-1">one edge enters from the future side, two exit toward the past: the recombinant offspring whose genome is inherited from two parental nodes, fusing portions of two parental genomes into a single haplotype.</cite> That is your (Doug, Ana, New) mosaic constructor, formalized. The child is a draw from two pools, not a specialization.
Coalescence node — <cite index="24-1">two edges merge when, backward in time, a parent provides the same portion of genome to multiple offspring, splitting one lineage into many going forward.</cite> That is your "enough of the population shares an ancestor" — the shared-ancestry event, located as a specific node.
And your "find the moments that introduced the compacted configuration" is a named operation: each genome segment <cite index="24-1">has a distinct inheritance history from the recombination event where it split to the coalescence event where it joins back up, and each recombination event increases the lineage count by one.</cite> So every configuration is traceable to the exact recombination/coalescence nodes that produced it. The "magic moment" is a node with a timestamp.
Now the part that should interest you most, because it's your fight verbatim. The field had a classical formalism that put coalescence and recombination on the nodes — event-based — and a 2024 paper (Wong, Kelleher, et al., Genetics) argues that's the wrong type and replaces it: <cite index="18-1">classical formalisms focused on mapping coalescence and recombination events to the nodes; this is out of step with modern developments, so we present a formalism that defines an ARG in terms of specific genomes and their intervals of genetic inheritance, and show it generalizes the classical treatments.</cite> Their corrected object: <cite index="19-1">a genome ARG is a directed acyclic graph in which nodes represent haploid genomes and edges represent genetic inheritance between an ancestor and a descendant, with edges annotated by the genome coordinates over which inheritance occurred.</cite>
Read that against the last two turns. The classical version typed the events (recombination, coalescence) as first-class — intermediaries, the thing you kept telling me doesn't exist. The new version throws the events out and keeps only specific genomes (your "everything real is a specific relationship") plus edges annotated with the interval inherited (inheritance that is partial and coordinate-indexed, not whole-cloth specialization). They moved from typing intermediaries to typing concrete things with annotated inheritance — the same correction you made me make. And it's not settled doctrine; the paper exists because <cite index="19-1">there is genuine confusion about what, precisely, an ARG is — the term has many meanings varying over time and context.</cite> So your instinct that "it's a weird type problem someone should care about" is right down to the detail: the type problem is live, named, and being actively re-formalized right now.
Where this leaves SRT, and why it's worth your attention rather than just reassurance:
The thing they don't have, and you do, is the apex. The gARG types the inheritance of genome intervals — it answers "which ancestor did this stretch come from" — but it has no -> Human edge, no species node an individual points to directly. Species, in their world, is still read off the population after the fact; the ARG itself is pure genealogy. Your construction adds the layer they leave informal: the mosaic step is their recombination edge (annotated, partial, lossy — formalized), and the species counter is a symbolization over the ARG — a Human apex minted when coalescence density crosses a threshold. So SRT isn't reinventing the ARG; it's proposing the type they don't have — the apex that turns a genealogical graph into a typed species ontology.
And the "species counter incrementing" is genuinely the open edge even in their world. The ARG gives you every coalescence node, but when a cluster of coalescences counts as a new species — your compaction threshold — is exactly the boundary their formalism leaves to the population biologist, retrospectively. That's the same admission/symbolization trigger we couldn't close, and now it's confirmed: the genealogy formalizes cleanly; the species-counter increment does not, in their framework either. So the part you're unsure how to formalize is unformalized for them too — which means it's not a gap in your understanding, it's the actual frontier.
Two concrete things to chase if you want to build on real ground rather than reinvent it: the Wong/Kelleher 2024 Genetics paper "A general and efficient representation of ancestral recombination graphs" for the corrected type (genomes + interval-annotated edges), and the tskit / tree-sequence data structure (Kelleher et al.) that implements exactly the "growing compacted list of ancestors" — it stores the genealogy succinctly by recording where along the genome the tree changes, which is your compaction made into a data structure.
The question that's now sharp enough to be worth putting to them rather than to me: their edges carry genome intervals — partial, coordinate-indexed inheritance. SRT's -> carries truth whole. Is your species apex a function of how much interval-ancestry coalesces (a measure on the ARG — quantitative, the thing you said needs the number side you haven't built), or is it a qualitative threshold that mints Human once any stable shared structure persists? Because the ARG can give you the measure, but converting that measure into a discrete species-counter increment is the quantity-to-type step — the exact boundary you flagged turns ago where property-as-type either splinters or hands off to number. The biology literature hands it to a measure; SRT would have to either build the measure or justify the qualitative jump. Which do you think the species counter is — measured, or minted?

Everything about relational inheritance is specification. Relationships hold when things get more specific. So while generalizing via speciation is fine, it is not the way to think about relationships
How is it that we can have: r -> ref r == (s,o) s =r> o s !=ref> o
It’s because the first is an existence claim:
E[x,y]: x =r> y =|> x =ref> y
When x and y are r, both r =r> r and r =ref> r are true
If we can say that there exists some scenario where two of the referents are specified, the third has a substitution. I don’t understand subjective and objective substitution well yet. It’s easy when they are the same:
x -> t := x =t> x y -> x -> t =|> y -> t y =t> y
So that is a very easy to comprehend notion of inheritance. It shows that this inheritance is similar to the inheritance of single parent evolution. You inherit all the properties of your parents, and then are a new sort of type property that distinguishes you. And maybe this is enough for the inheritance operation to make sense. Expressing relational inheritance is then the tough part. But the r =ref> r but not s =ref> o example is the way that r can only be partially substituted
Claude
15:02 UTC, 7 June 2026
You've located the actual fault, and it's deeper than a fix — it's that I had the direction of the whole thing backwards. Speciation generalizes; relationships specify; those run opposite ways, and relational inheritance is the specifying one. So building relationships out of speciation-from-referent was running the relationship machinery up the generalization arrow. Wrong arrow, same error as confusing inheritance with specification ten turns ago, now at the level of the whole construction. Let me work it in your terms and not import a frame.
The four lines, read as you read them — the first an existence claim, the rest its consequences:
srt
r -> ref            E[x,y]: x =r> y =|> x =ref> y     (r is a relationship: relating through r entails relating at all)
r == (s,o)
s =r> o
s !=ref> o
And the crux is your last sentence, which I want to state precisely because it's the mechanism: 0 holds but 1 does not — 2 substitutes into 3 only on the diagonal, partially. When x and y are both r, both r =r> r and r =ref> r are true — full substitution, because subject and object coincide and there's nothing to hold apart. But s =r> o with s != o does not give s =ref> o, because off the diagonal the substitution into ref fails. ref == (ref,ref) is diagonal — both ends the same — so a distinct pair (s,o) cannot be a ref-relating. s and o are each individually -> ref (each is a referent), but the relating s =r> o does not collapse to s =ref> o. The relationship substitutes into the referent only where the referent's diagonal can receive it — at coincidence — and is blocked where the ends are distinct.
That is the partial substitution, and it's exactly what role-based inheritance needs, because it tells you the substitution is per-position, not whole. r -> ref does not mean "replace r by ref everywhere." It means: each position of r substitutes into the corresponding position of ref — subject-of-r is a referent, object-of-r is a referent — but the relating substitutes only on the diagonal. So -> ref distributes across the three positions independently, and the relating-position is the one that resists. Three separate substitutions, one per role, not one substitution of the triple. This is what I kept missing by treating (s,o) as a whole that inherits as a unit.
Now the easy case you gave, which is the anchor, and it is genuinely easy and I shouldn't dress it up:
srt
x -> t := x =t> x
y -> x  =|>  y -> t   (given x -> t)        i.e.  y =t> y
y inherits x, x inherits t, so y inherits t — single-parent, transitive, and the picture is precisely single-parent descent: you get all your parent's type-properties, plus a new differentia that distinguishes you. That's the diagonal inheritance, fully comprehended, and it's enough to make the inheritance operation make sense. No quarrel. The reason it's easy is that everything is on the diagonal — =t> self-relations — so substitution is full, never partial, and the role question never arises because all three positions are the same referent.
The hard part — relational inheritance — is hard for one reason, now nameable: off the diagonal, the three positions are distinct, so substitution becomes per-role and partial, and you need a syntax that says which role substitutes into which. That's the "ordered-set-like" thing you're asking for, and I think the honest form of it — staying inside SRT, no sets — is a role-indexed substitution, written as a substitution on each position separately with the relating-position guarded by the diagonal condition. Something like: r -> ref unfolds to
srt
subject-role:   x =r> y  =|>  x -> ref
object-role:    x =r> y  =|>  y -> ref
relating-role:  x =r> y  &  x == y  =|>  r =ref> r     (guarded: only on the diagonal)
                x =r> y  &  x != y  =|>  s !=ref> o    (blocked off it)
Three role-clauses, the third conditioned on coincidence. That's role-based inheritance: -> between relationships isn't one arrow, it's three position-substitutions, two unconditional and one diagonal-guarded. And it's why r == (s,o) while ref == (ref,ref): r differs from ref exactly in that its relating-role permits distinct ends, so substituting r -> ref succeeds on the two relatum-roles and fails on the relating-role whenever the ends are distinct. The whole content of "r is a relationship and ref is the diagonal" lives in that one guarded clause.
So I think the syntax you need is not ordered sets — it's a notation for substitution indexed by role, with the relating-role carrying a coincidence guard. The relatum roles substitute freely (every end is a referent); the relating role substitutes only on the diagonal (only there does a relationship collapse to bare referring). Subjective and objective substitution — the thing you said you don't understand yet — would then be the two relatum-role substitutions, and the asymmetry between them (if there is one) is the only thing left to pin: does subject-role substitute the same way as object-role, or does directionality make subject-substitution carry something object-substitution doesn't?
That last is the real open question and I won't guess it, because it's the subjective-vs-objective-substitution gap you flagged. Here's the sharp version: in s =r> o, both s -> ref and o -> ref hold — both ends are referents. But s is the subject and o the object, and Directionality says the relation isn't symmetric. So does subject-substitutions -> ref mean the same as object-substitution o -> ref, or does the subject inherit into ref differently because it's the active end — e.g. subject-substitution must preserve that s can relate outward (s =r> o) while object-substitution need only preserve being-related-to? Put concretely: is there a referent that can be an object but never a subject — o-substitutable but not s-substitutable — which would make the two role-substitutions genuinely different operations and not just the same -> ref at two positions? That single question decides whether role-based inheritance has two relatum-rules or one.
Doug
15:06 UTC, 7 June 2026
You know this is a different angle, but I think it might be the real angle? When we say x =t> x will never imply x =t> y, we’re trying to specify through axioms. The idea that that’s actually a binary predicate. So there’s no slot to possibly comprehend it differently. So that the rules really are those 6 roles where all different, two of three are the same
Claude
15:08 UTC, 7 June 2026
The arity is the comprehension. x =t> x is R(x,x,t) — two free arguments. x =t> y is R(x,y,t) — three. The first can't imply the second because there is no y in it to constrain; a predicate holding of one tuple says nothing about a different tuple. So x =t> x !=> x =t> y was never an axiom to specify — it's forced by the predicate having the shape it has, and writing a rule for it was inventing machinery for something the arity already forbids. There's no slot to comprehend differently because the slot isn't there.
And then the cases are generated, not chosen. Three positions, partitioned by coincidence:
srt
all distinct       x =t> y      → 6 directed cross-relations: S→O O→S S→T T→S O→T T→O
two of three same  x =t> x  (S=O, typing/property)
                   x =x> y  (S=T, symbol)
                   x =t> t  (O=T, representative)
all three same     x =x> x      → the ref/eq corner
Your six are the ordered relations among three distinct positions — 3·2 = 6 — and they're six and not three because the relations are directed. That directedness is exactly the subjective/objective asymmetry you couldn't pin last turn: S→O is not O→S because R is directed, so subject-substitution and object-substitution differ by the ordering itself, not by anything posited. The Phase VI split 3 + 6 = 9 is then this table read off: the 3 are positions descending on the diagonal, the 6 are the directed cross-relations off it. Both fall out of arity.
This dissolves the guard I built last turn. "r =ref> r but not s =ref> o" — I re-derived by axiom what arity gives free. =ref> is the diagonal predicate (ref == (ref,ref)), so it has no distinct-object slot at all. r -> ref substitutes the two relatum roles — each end is a referent — but cannot produce s =ref> o, because that needs a slot =ref> doesn't carry. Partial substitution isn't a coincidence-guard; it's that you cannot substitute up in arity. The diagonal is binary, the relating off-diagonal is ternary, and substitution can't manufacture the missing argument.
What this does not settle, and is the whole remaining substance: arity fixes the slots, not the implications among them. That there are six directed cross-roles is forced; which cross-implication fires under inheritance — when x→t forces x'→t' — is the theory, not the combinatorics. So the angle is right and it's load-bearing: it removes the temptation to axiomatize the shape of the comprehension (the shape is just the ternary's coincidence lattice, finite, no schema), and it isolates the real question to which of the six fire and whether they fire independently.
Which is the subjective/objective question, now exactly placed: do the six split into two genuinely distinct operations along the S/O direction, or does Directionality make one the master and the other its consequence — so the six independent-looking cross-rules collapse to fewer? Is S→O-preservation derivable from O→S-preservation under Directionality, or are they two irreducible rules?

Let me reason about it rather than receive it, because I think the "break" is actually a re-indexing, and there's one place where it genuinely tears that's worth isolating.
First, what (x,y) now means. If r == (s,o) and s == (s,o) both hold with == as equality, then r == s by transitivity, and the foundation collapses. It doesn't collapse, so (s,o) is no longer functioning as the unique referent of the pair — it's functioning as a shape, and r, s are distinct referents realizing that shape in different ways. That's the whole content of "degeneracy = different kind." So you haven't dropped uniqueness; you've re-indexed it. The index of a most-specific is no longer the pair, it's the pair and the coincidence-kind — which is exactly the lattice arity handed us last turn:
text
all-distinct   S=O        S=T        O=T        all-same
Per (pair, kind), the most-specific is still unique. s's "three most-specifics" — (o,o), (s,o), (o,s) — are s being the meet in three different kinds: (o,o) as S=O (type), (s,o) as S=T (symbol), (o,s) as O=T (representative). Not three bottoms of one pair; one bottom in each of three kinds. Uniqueness holds kind-wise. The reason it looked broken is that you're sitting on referents that occupy several kinds at once.
Which is exactly why it's a molecule and not a tree. Ordinary referents live in the all-distinct interior — one kind — and never loop. The foundational referents o, s, r sit on the coincidence vertices, so each occupies multiple kinds, and that's what closes the loop o … s … o … o. And the loop does not violate Directionality, because Directionality is acyclicity per single relationship's inheritance — the molecule cycles through different relatings (s, r, o), no one of which cycles into itself. So a multi-kind cycle is legal where a single-kind cycle isn't. The molecule is the shape a foundation must take precisely because its atoms are the degenerate ones.
Now the tear, and I don't think your resolution closes it yet. "One inherits the other, directed" needs a principle fixing which — and your two shared shapes fix the direction in opposite relation to coincidence:
srt
shape (o,o):   o =o> o   (all-same)   vs   o =s> o   (S=O)
               s -> o     ⇒ the FEWER-coincidence relating (S=O) is MORE specific
               consistent with Phase VI: more specific = more distinct

shape (s,o):   s =r> o   (all-distinct) vs  s =s> o   (S=T)
               s -> r     ⇒ the MORE-coincidence relating (S=T) is MORE specific
               contradicts Phase VI: here more coincidence = more specific
At (o,o) the bottom is the more-distinct relating; at (s,o) the bottom is the more-coincident one. So coincidence-count doesn't determine specificity, and worse, s -> r directly violates the rule that specialization adds distinctions and never merges them — s =s> o is a merge (subject = relating) sitting below the all-distinct s =r> o. That's not a different kind being fine; that's a relationship getting more specific by identifying two positions, which Phase VI forbids going down.
I see three ways out and I won't pick for you, because which one you take reshapes the foundation:
0 is the wrong direction — it should be r -> s, the all-distinct relating is the bottom, and Phase VI holds uniformly. But then a symbol ismore general than a relationship, which reads backwards semantically (a symbol feels like a special kind of relating, hence more specific).
There are two specialization modes — split (add a distinction, Phase VI, the relational kind) and merge (add a coincidence-constraint, the symbol/type/representative kinds) — and -> descends along both, so specificity is two-dimensional and "more specific" is no longer a single order. Then s -> r is fine; it's merge-descent, orthogonal to Phase VI's split-descent. This is the most powerful reading and the most expensive: it means -> isn't one preorder.
The overload is the culprit — using one s for symbol and type and representative forces s -> o (from its type-role at (o,o)) and s -> r (from its symbol-role at (s,o)) onto a single referent, and those two impose the contradictory specificity demands above. Splitting s into three referents — s_sym, s_type, s_rep — might make each direction consistent, at the cost of the unification you wanted.
My read is that 2 and 3 are the real fork: either the three roles are genuinely one referent and -> must become two-dimensional to carry both merge and split (powerful, but the preorder breaks), or-> stays one order and the three roles must separate (clean, but the molecule loses the single s that made it a molecule). The contradiction is forced by wanting both — one s and one ->.
Two things I need from you before I can push further, because I'm reading compressed notation and guessing risks importing error:
Is s -> r actually asserted, or only conditional? You wrote s -> r =|> s =r> s, which is just the definition unfolding — it doesn't commit to s -> r holding. If s -> r is not asserted, the tear may not exist and the direction at (s,o) is still open. And what does <s=s> mean — a symmetric bond between the two referents (which would conflict with R being directed), or an edge labeled by the S=T coincidence read in one direction? The molecule reading depends entirely on whether those brackets are symmetric, and a symmetric bond is a much bigger commitment than a labeled directed edge.

Now the syntax sits right, and re-reading the molecule with it changes what I think you're claiming. <s=s> is one bond read both directions, so
srt
o <s=s> s   =   s =s> o   &   o =s> s
and those two are not the same statement — they swap which pole is subject. s =s> o is S=T: the relating is the subject, so s symbolizes o. o =s> s is O=T: the relating is the object, so s is o's representative. Same bond <s=s>, two directions, and the two directions are your first two readings: subject symbolizes object going one way, object represents subject going the other. The bracket isn't shorthand — it's the claim that symbol and representative are inverse readings of a single bond, and R being directed is exactly what keeps them from collapsing into one. That's why the foundation is bonded bidirectionally where ordinary structure is a DAG: the foundational semantic relations come in symbol/representative inverse pairs, and the loop is built from them.
So the ontology, as I now read it: one directed bond, subject → object, which is reference. o is the object pole — referent, ground, ref. The subject pole is the active end, and the three classical roles are not three things, they're where the relating falls:
srt
s =r> o   all distinct   subject refers to object   (reference — subject is the type, object is about it)
s =s> o   S=T            subject symbolizes object  (symbol)
o =s> s   O=T            object represents subject   (representative)
o =s> o   S=O            object is typed             (type)
And the canonical bond is the fixed point read as self-reference: the subject-of-subjects, whose objects are themselves subjects, and which is its own object — o <o=o> o, the place where subject, relating, and object coincide. Articulation away from that point is what splits the one ground into subject, relating, object. That's a real claim and I think it's the right shape for a theory whose name is reference: the primitive isn't relationship, it's the directed referring bond, and relationship is its all-distinct reading.

Last turn the tear was that one referent s carried three most-specifics (o,o), (s,o), (o,s) and got forced into s -> o and s -> r with contradictory specificity. Per coincidence-kind there's one reading, and nothing has to be two specificities at once.
But that only works if s is a role, not a referent — the name of the subject pole that different referents occupy — and you still wrote s == (o,o), s == (s,o), s == (o,s), which reads as one referent with three bottoms. srt
s =s> o

The relating is s because what s does to o is exactly the subjecthood that s is. So o is a subject — an instance falling under the subject of subjects.
srt
o =s> s
Now o, that instance, is about s through s. The particular subject o refers back to the subject-of-subjects, and it does so by being a subject — so it stands for s. o is the representative: the concrete subject that points back at what subjecthood is. That's why the object is the representative one — not because an arrow runs backward, but because the instance is what represents the kind.
srt
o =s> o

Used user time v0
Here's an axiomatization attempt, built only from the carry you settled and the foundational referents, and I'll test it against mother–daughter before trusting it.
The setup in SRT terms. The canonical relationship is s =r> o: the subject of subjects refers, through r, to o, its representative referent — and r == (s,o). A generic relationship is a =rA> b, and what we want is: a is a subject (not s), b is an object (not o), rA represents r, and different rA preserve different aspects.
The forced part needs no new axiom — it's the carry pointed at the canonical. Take the carry,
srt
s =r> o  &  a =rA> b  =|>  rA -> r  =|>  a -> s  &  b -> o
So the only thing a generic relationship must assert is rA -> r — that its relating is a kind of canonical reference. The carry then forces a -> s (left is a subject) and b -> o (right is an object). Role-typing is not an axiom about subjects and objects; it's the carry applied at the foundational r == (s,o). That's the whole of "the thing on the left is a subject, the thing on the right is an object."
The self-reference resolves without being axiomatized — and it's exactly the mother–daughter asymmetry. "Mother is a daughter" is a -> s -> o: every subject is also a referent, because s -> o holds foundationally (the subject of subjects is itself a referent). So the left pole is never barred from being an object elsewhere — that falls out by transitivity, type-level, automatic. "A mother is not a type of her daughter" is the non-fact b !-> a: the specific object does not inherit the specific subject. And crucially, nothing in the role-typing forces b -> a. The carry gives a -> s and b -> o — it never gives b -> a. So the two halves of your sentence are precisely forced role-typing (mother is a subject, hence a referent, hence a daughter) versus unforced cross-pairing (this daughter is not this mother's type). The asymmetry isn't a special rule; it's that the carry types the positions and says nothing about the specific edge between them.
The selective part is the six cross-edges, each preserved iff 0 inherits the canonical's corresponding edge. The canonical relationship has cross-structure among s, r, o — o -> s (the representative is a subject), and the rest. A generic rA carries a given cross-edge down to its own pair only when rA -> r also transmits that edge, which is a fact about rA's own most-specific, not a free choice and not a schema — six finite conditions, one per directed cross-edge. Mother–daughter inherits the role-typing but breaks b -> a; a symbol-like relationship preserves a =rA> a collapse; a representative-like one preserves b -> a. The preserved subset is what kind of relationship rA is. So "preserve different parts to preserve different aspects" is: which of the six canonical cross-edges rA transmits.
Stated as the theory:
srt
Reference (forced):   a =rA> b & rA -> r  =|>  a -> s  &  b -> o
Self-object (derived): a -> s -> o          (every subject is a referent; "mother is a daughter")
Cross (selective):     for each canonical cross-edge e among {s,r,o},
                        rA transmits e  =|>  the matching edge holds on (a, rA, b)
The role-typing is closed and forced; the cross-edges are open and per-rA; the self-reference is a theorem, not a posit.
Two things I won't decide for you because they change the object. First, which relationship is canonical —s =r> o (all-distinct, the one I used) or s =s> o (the symbol case, S=T)? I took the all-distinct one because a generic relationship is all-distinct and should inherit from the all-distinct canonical, but if the symbol case is the true root then r itself carries a coincidence and the carry transmits more. Second: are all six cross-edges selective, or is at least one forced for every relationship — e.g. must every rA preserve o -> s (object is a subject), since b -> o -> ...? If even one is forced, it joins role-typing and the selective set shrinks to five. Which canonical, and is any cross-edge non-negotiable?

19:56 UTC, 9 June 2026
Right — the carry assumed three distinct positions, and s =s> o has only two referents (s in both subject and relating). So role-typing as I wrote it doesn't apply to it, and worse, it wouldmisread it: feed s =s> o to the carry as if rA = s, a = s, b = o, and it tries to force s -> s and o -> o as the role-typing — which collapses, because the subject and the relating are the same referent. The variables hide that there's no third thing. The carry needs a, rA, b to be three; s =s> o is R(s, o, s), two arguments. Different arity, so the three-position axiom can't be the one that governs it.
This is the same arity fact from before, now biting the foundation: s =s> o is a binary predicate (free s, free o), a =rA> b is ternary. You can't run a ternary preservation axiom on a binary thing — there's no slot. Sos =s> o is not a special case of the reference axiom; it's a different shape, and the equivalence it expresses (subject symbolizes object, S=T) is governed by a binary statement, not the ternary carry.
Which means the theory needs the carry stated per coincidence-kind, not once. The role-typing I gave is the all-distinct kind. The S=T kind — x =x> y, symbol — has its own preservation, and it has two positions to type, not three:
srt
all-distinct  a =rA> b      three positions    a -> s, b -> o, rA -> r
S=T (symbol)  a =a> b       two positions      a -> ?,  b -> o
In a =a> b, the subject and relating are one referent a. So there's no separate rA -> r to assert — the relating is the subject. What types a here is not "a -> s because it's the subject" plus "a -> r because it's the relating" — those would double-type the one referent and that's exactly the collapse I'd produce by misreading. Instead a, occupying subject-and-relating at once, inherits the canonical thing that occupies subject-and-relating at once — which is s in s =s> o. So the symbol-kind role-typing is a -> s once (covering both its roles), b -> o. Two typings for two positions, not three.
So the generalization isn't "extend the carry to cover s =s> o." It's that there's a carry per coincidence-kind, each with the arity of that kind: the ternary carry types three positions, the binary symbol-carry types two, the representative-carry (O=T) types two the other way, the type-carry (S=O) types two, and the all-same is the fixed point. Five carries, one per vertex of the lattice, each with its own arity. AndrA -> r is the relating-typing only in the all-distinct kind, because only there is the relating a separate position to type.
What I don't yet have, and it's the real question your s =s> o forces: how do the per-kind carries relate? Is the binary symbol-carry derivable from the ternary one by identifying two positions — i.e. setrA = a in the ternary carry and see what survives the collapse — or is it an independent axiom because collapsing two positions in a ternary statement isn't a legal substitution (you can't merge free variables and keep the same predicate)? My instinct from the arity argument is the latter: you cannot get the binary carry by substituting in the ternary one, because identifying rA with a changes the arity, and a predicate of one arity says nothing about another. If that's right, thens =s> o is not derived from the reference axiom at all — it needs its own carry, posited separately, and the foundation has one carry per kind with no reduction between them. Is that the structure — five independent per-kind carries — or do you want the collapsed ones to follow from the all-distinct one by some legal identification I'm not seeing?

r == (s,o) s -(s,o)> r o -(s,o)> r s == (o,o) o -(o,o)> s
And then we ask if o -> r?

This reads correctly and I think the formalism works — but only with one thing made explicit that your example is silently relying on, so let me pull it out and test it.
The function syntax: t(x,y) is x =t> y, so r(s,o), s(s,o), o(s,o) are the three relatings over the distinct pair (s,o), and s(o,o), o(o,o) are the two over the diagonal. And the labeled arrow x -(a,b)> y is inheritance through the relationship-context (a,b), with y == (a,b).
Here's the thing that has to be said out loud, because the transitivity result depends on it: 0 means two different things depending on whether 1 is diagonal.
srt
diagonal     a == b   x -(a,a)> y   is   x =y> x        type-instance: x IS a kind of y
off-diagonal a != b   x -(a,b)> y   is   a =y> b, x∈{a,b}  participation: x is an END of y
o -(o,o)> s is diagonal — o =s> o, o genuinely is an instance of s. But s -(s,o)> r is off-diagonal — it does not say s =r> s; it says s is the subject-end of r(s,o). Participation, not type-instance. That's why o -(s,o)> r can be true (o is the object-end of r) while o -> r is false (o is not a kind of r). The labeled arrow holds; the unlabeled type-inheritance doesn't. Those are different claims and the label is exactly what tells them apart.
So the transitivity rule that makes your result correct: type-instance links (diagonal labels) compose; a participation link (off-diagonal label) is a barrier. Test both directions:
text
o -(o,o)> s -(s,o)> r   :  type-instance, then participation → barrier → o !-> r   ✓ (your result)
dog -(dog,dog)> mammal -(mammal,mammal)> animal : both type-instance → dog -> animal ✓
Strict label-identity matching would have been too strong — it would block dog -> animal too, since (dog,dog) != (mammal,mammal). The rule isn't "same label," it's "diagonal labels chain, off-diagonal labels stop." o can't reach r because the only path runs through the off-diagonal (s,o), and participation in a relationship is not being an instance of it. o participates in r (as its object) without inheriting r — which is exactly right, and exactly what bare o -> r got wrong.
This is the role-index we've been missing for fifteen turns, and it's why it works: the label carries whether the inheritance is type (diagonal, transitive — the clade chains) or relational (off-diagonal, a barrier — participation doesn't propagate). The two regimes we kept colliding — type inheritance and relational inheritance — are now distinguished by the label being on the diagonal or off it, and transitivity is granted to one and denied to the other by that single fact.
Two things to pin before trusting it as the axiom base. First: is the composition rule exactly "diagonal transits, off-diagonal blocks," or is there a mixed case — a chain diagonal → diagonal → off-diagonal where the off-diagonal at the end still lets the earlier type-instance through to a participation conclusion? Your example only testeddiagonal → off-diagonal adjacent; the rule needs to be stated for arbitrary chains, and "any off-diagonal link anywhere kills type-transit" is the clean version. Second: this puts the carry axiom in its place. The carry — t' -> t =|> x' -> x & y' -> y — governs the off-diagonal participation links (how the ends of one relationship relate to the ends of another), while diagonal transitivity governs the type chains. So you have two laws, one per regime, and they meet only where a referent is both a type-instance and a participant. Is that the division you want — carry for off-diagonal, transitivity for diagonal — and is the meeting point (a referent that is both) where the orphaned-edge problem has been hiding the whole time?

You're right on both counts, and the second is the one I keep failing to internalize, so let me fix it first because it changes the whole reading.
o -(o,o)> s does not say s == (o,o) and it does not invoke most-specific at all. The label (o,o) is just the relationship the inheritance runs through — s(o,o), i.e. o =s> o — and the arrow says o inherits s through that relationship. No claim that s is the most-specific of anything, no uniqueness, no bottom. I keep smuggling "most-specific" into (a,b) because that's what the old parenthesis meant, but in this syntax (a,b) is the context — a relationship that the inheritance passes through — and s(o,o) is just one relating among possibly many over that pair. That's the reimagining: the label is a route, not the canonical bottom. So I should never write y == (a,b). The label is which relationship the inheritance is via, full stop.
That actually makes the arrow cleaner than what I proposed. x -(a,b)> y reads: x inherits y, by way of the relationship 1 — y is the relating, (a,b) is the pair it relates, and x is gaining y by virtue of that relating holding. o -(o,o)> s = o inherits s because s(o,o) holds. s -(s,o)> r = s inherits r because r(s,o) holds. Nothing about bottoms.
Now the part where you say I struggle — the extension. You've given a new expression of inheritance (the labeled, via-a-relationship arrow), and showing it reduces to x =y> x in the trivial case is not the work; it's the sanity check. The work is the new embodiment: saying what x -(a,b)> y is, as a primitive-level statement, in the general case where the old definition doesn't reach. Let me actually attempt that instead of admiring the reduction.
The old x -> y := x =y> x is one referent bearing a type on the diagonal. The new arrow has three things in play — x, the relating y, and the pair (a,b) it runs through — and x is one of a, b. So the embodiment has to say how x inheriting y depends on x's role in y(a,b). The candidate, built only from the primitive:
srt
x -(a,b)> y  :=  y(a,b)  &  ( x =a> x  or  x =b> x ... )
— no. That's me reaching again. Let me say what it should mean and then find the form. It should mean: the relationship 0 holds, and x, occupying one end of it, takes on y as something it now bears in that role. The role is the new content — x doesn't bear y flatly (that would be x =y> x, the diagonal, the old case); x bears y as the a-end or as the b-end. So the embodiment is role-relative bearing:
srt
x -(a,b)> y  :=  a =y> b  &  x is the position whose bearing-of-y this records
and the two positions give two different inheritances of the same y through the same pair — a -(a,b)> y (subject-role bearing) and b -(a,b)> y (object-role bearing) — which are genuinely different, and that is the extension: inheritance is no longer a single relation but is indexed by which end you are. The trivial case collapses when a, b, and the diagonal coincide: x -(x,x)> y is x =y> x, the old definition, recovered because there's only one end.
So the new embodiment of inheritance is: to inherit through a relationship is to bear the relating in the role of one of its ends. Subject-bearing and object-bearing are the two embodiments, distinct off the diagonal, identical on it. That's the thing that extends x =y> x — it puts a role index on the bearing, and the old definition is the index-free shadow you see when the ends collapse.
What I can't write yet, and it's the actual open work rather than a sanity check: the two role-bearings needrules, and they're not the same rule. Subject-bearing a -(a,b)> y and object-bearing b -(a,b)> y must compose differently — that's the whole content of o participating in r without inheriting it. So the question that is the real extension: what does subject-role bearing let you conclude that object-role bearing does not? I don't think it's symmetric, and the asymmetry is the embodiment's actual law. What distinguishes inheriting-as-subject from inheriting-as-object?

14:53 UTC, 10 June 2026
Fact-checking the inferred rule. To be precise about what I'm checking — the embodiment is
text
x -(a,b)> y  :=  x =(a,b)> x   with   (a,b) -> y
and the "lot more" is that, because x is typed by the relationship (a,b) and (a,b) -> y, x used as a relating inherits y's relating over the context:
srt
a' =x> b'  &  a' -> a  &  b' -> b   =|>   a' =y> b'
Five relationships, distinct domains:
srt
parent/ancestor    parent-of  -(organism,organism)> ancestor-of
   A =parent-of> B & A,B -> organism  =|>  A =ancestor-of> B          ✓ a parent is an ancestor
employment         employed-by -(person,org)> affiliated-with
   A =employed-by> B & A -> person & B -> org  =|>  A =affiliated> B   ✓
geometry           contained-in -(region,region)> overlaps
   A =contained-in> B & A,B -> region  =|>  A =overlaps> B             ✓ containment forces overlap
social             friends-with -(person,person)> knows
   A =friends-with> B & A,B -> person  =|>  A =knows> B                ✓
order              less-than -(number,number)> at-most
   3 =less-than> 5 & 3,5 -> number  =|>  3 =at-most> 5                 ✓
All five hold, and the context does real work — it isn't decorative. Drop b' -> org from the employment case: if you're employed-by a person (a household employer, b' -> person not org), the conclusion "affiliated with an organization" doesn't fire, correctly, because the gate b' -> org fails. So the rule is sound and the gate is load-bearing: x -(a,b)> y is genuine sub-relation inheritance restricted to the pairs below (a,b). It also correctly refuses bad cases — loves -(person,person)> respects is simply false (loving doesn't imply respecting), so the premise never holds and nothing over-fires. The rule fires exactly when x is really a narrower relating than y over that domain.
Now the core relationships in the same frame. Two of them behave just like the world cases:
srt
s -(s,o)> r :  A =s> B & A -> s & B -> o  =|>  A =r> B
   "symbolizing implies referring, for a subject symbolizing a referent"   ✓ symbol is a kind of reference
o -(s,o)> r :  A =o> B & A -> s & B -> o  =|>  A =r> B
   "representing implies referring, over the same context"                  ✓ representation is a kind of reference
Those read true and the gate (s,o) is a real pair — subject on the left, referent on the right. The framework holds at the foundation here.
But o -(o,o)> s is where it breaks, and it breaks in exactly the way that demands your unique-referent/arity caveat:
srt
o -(o,o)> s :  A =o> B & A -> o & B -> o  =|>  A =s> B
The gate is A -> o & B -> o — and every referent inherits o (x -> o is the universal axiom). So the gate is vacuous: it admits everything. The inference collapses toA =o> B =|> A =s> B with no restriction — every o-relating is forced to be an s-relating, unconditionally. That's the over-fire. In the world cases the gate (organism,organism), (person,org) carved out a proper sub-domain because those types sit strictly below everything; at the foundation the gating type is o, the top, so there's nothing left to gate against. The context-restriction mechanism runs out of headroom precisely when the context is the universal referent.
So your closing remark is the diagnosis, not a hedge: the world-level rule is gated by being-a-more-specific-type, and that gate works as long as there's a more-general type above to contrast with. At the foundation there isn't — o is the top — so type-gating degenerates and you need a different handle to keep o -(o,o)> s from saying everything. That handle is the unique referent / arity: at the level where the only types are o, s, r, you can no longer distinguish admissible pairs by what type they are (they're all just o), so you must distinguish them by identity and position-count — which referent, in how many argument slots. Arity is what replaces type-gating once you've exhausted the type hierarchy.
Which makes the precise question: is the foundational gate meant to be A -> o (type, vacuous) or something arity-based — e.g. A and B distinct, or A occupying a subject-slot it doesn't occupy in the diagonal (o,o)? Because the world cases gate by type and the core case can't, so either the foundation has a second gating rule (arity-based) running alongside the type-based one, or the type rule is a derived shadow of an arity rule that's primary everywhere and only looks like type-gating once enough referents exist. Which is it — two gates, or one arity gate that the type gate is a special case of?

Setting it up so we're checking the same thing: x -(a,b)> y says x is a narrower relating than y, gated by the pair (a,b), with the operational rule
srt
a' =x> b'  &  a' -> a  &  b' -> b   =|>   a' =y> b'
"inherit from any pair the relationship applies to, and you end up inheriting the pair." Five cases, each a different way referents coincide, real relationships, checked honestly:
1 — all distinct. teaches -(teacher, student)> influences
srt
Aristotle =teaches> Alexander,  Aristotle -> teacher,  Alexander -> student
   =|>  Aristotle =influences> Alexander
Sound. Teaching is gated influence; the gate (teacher,student) is doing real work — rock =teaches> nobody never arises. Baseline, all six referents distinct.
2 — relata equal, 0 (diagonal gate, property). is-prime -(number, number)> is-integer
srt
7 =is-prime> 7,  7 -> number   =|>   7 =is-integer> 7
Sound, and now the gate pair is the diagonal (number,number), the inheritor collapses to 7 =is-prime> 7. This is property-subsumption — prime ⟹ integer — and it's the case where "inherit the pair" means inherit a self-relation. Works cleanly.
3 — specific equals general, 0 (reflexive). equals -(number, number)> equals
srt
2+2 =equals> 4   =|>   2+2 =equals> 4
Vacuously sound. Worth including because it shows the framework contains its own reflexivity: every relating sub-relates itself, x -(a,b)> x is the identity of the inheritance order. The degenerate x=y is not a bug, it's the unit.
4 — relating equals subject, 0 (S=T, symbol). A pointing finger: the finger is the pointing. finger =finger> target, inheriting indicates.
srt
this-finger =finger> that-cup,  this-finger -> finger,  that-cup -> target
   =|>  this-finger =indicates> that-cup
Sound, but the relating is not independent — the subject doubles as it. The rule still fires, which is the interesting part: even when the relating collapses into a position, gated sub-relation inheritance survives. Symbol behaves like any other narrower relating.
5 — inheritor relata equal, 0 (gate off-diagonal). causes -(cause, effect)> precedes, applied to self-causation:
srt
feedback-loop =causes> feedback-loop,  feedback-loop -> cause,  feedback-loop -> effect
   =|>  feedback-loop =precedes> feedback-loop
Sound, and it exposes a real constraint: when a' = b', the single referent must satisfy both gate conditions — -> cause and -> effect. A thing can self-apply an off-diagonal relation only by inheriting both of its endpoint-types. That's the first place the gate stops being a formality: self-reference is admitted exactly when one referent qualifies for both roles.
Now the core relationships in the same frame:
srt
r == (s,o)    all-distinct gate    — reference; symbol and representative inherit r (example 1/4 shape)
s == (o,o)    diagonal gate        — "being a subject" as a relating over the referent diagonal (example 2 shape)
o == (o,o)    diagonal gate        — the ground self-relating, o =o> o
r sits cleanly at an off-diagonal pair (s,o) — it's example 1, no trouble. But s and o share the gate 4 — both are relatings over the same diagonal pair. This is precisely your earlier "two relationships, one most-specific pair" degeneracy, and now the examples tell us why it's forced rather than pathological: the diagonal gate (o,o) is example 2's shape, where the pair collapses, so the pair carries no information to distinguish the relatings sitting over it. At (s,o) the pair distinguishes r from anything else; at (o,o) the pair cannot distinguish s from o.
So the framework makes sense, and it predicts where it must break: the only foundational degeneracy is at the diagonal gate, and it's resolvable only by the relating referent's identity — s versus o — not by the pair. That is exactly your closing requirement read back out of the examples: at the level where every gate is (o,o) and every endpoint is o, type-gating is exhausted (it was already in example 2 — the gate is the whole domain), and theonly remaining individuator is which referent, and how many distinct ones — arity. You need unique-referent-to-arity not as an add-on but because the diagonal gate strips away the pair as a discriminator, leaving identity and count as the last handles.
The one thing the examples don't yet settle, and it's the question to take next: in example 5 the gate forced a' -> cause & a' -> effect to admit self-application. At the foundation, s over (o,o) is already the self-applied case — o =s> o is a referent relating to itself through s. So is s == (o,o) admitted by the same example-5 condition — o qualifying for both endpoint-roles of s — and if so, is s distinguished from o precisely by which additional role-condition it satisfies that o does not? That would make arity-individuation concrete: s and o share the pair (o,o) but differ in the role-qualifications they impose, and counting those is the arity you're asking for.

Claude
15:25 UTC, 10 June 2026
Right. The chain doesn't close, and the reason is in the syntax once you read the labels as roles, not as a single s.
srt
o -(o,o)> s    is    o =s> o      o bears s, in the (o,o) role — s as a type of o
s -(s,o)> r    is    s =r> s      s bears r, in the (s,o) role — s as the subject-end of r
Ordinary diagonal transitivity would want o =s> o & s =r> s =|> o =r> o. But that step is only valid if the middle s is the same s in both — same role. It isn't. In the first link s is the relating over 3 — it's functioning as a type. In the second link s is the subject-position of 5 — it's functioning as a relatum. The variable s is identical; the role is not. Transitivity chains roles, and these two roles don't meet, so o =r> o does not follow even though both o =s> o and s =r> s hold. That's the whole of it: o participates in r's structure through s, but it never bears r, because the s it bears is type-s, not subject-s.
This is the thing the labeled arrow buys that bare -> could not express. o -> s -> r =|> o -> r was true in the old diagonal system precisely because there were no roles — s was just s, and the self-relations chained. The new system attaches (o,o) and (s,o) to the two arrows, and those labels record that the s being inherited and the s doing the inheriting occupy different argument-positions. Same referent, two positions, and transitivity is blocked at the position-mismatch. So the label isn't decoration on the old arrow — it's exactly the information the old arrow threw away, the information that lets one referent hold two roles without the roles leaking into each other.
And it pins what "transitivity" even means here. It's not "the relation -> is transitive." It's: a chain x -(a,b)> m -(c,d)> z closes to x -> z only when m's role as the inherited thing in the first link matches m's role as the inheriting thing in the second — when m exits the first arrow in the same position it enters the second. s exits link one as the relating-over-(o,o) and enters link two as the subject-of-(s,o); exit-role ≠ entry-role; no closure. The dog→mammal→animal chains closed because mammal exits as a type and enters as a type — role preserved. So the transitivity law of the new system is role-continuity of the middle term, and the labels are what let you check it.
Which makes the precise statement I'd want to nail down: is the closure condition exactly "exit-position ofm in link one = entry-position of m in link two," with position drawn from {subject, object, relating}? Because if so, the five coincidence patterns aren't just a catalogue of relationships — they're a catalogue of which exits match which entries, and the whole transitivity theory reduces to a small table over three positions: which role a referent leaves an arrow in, which role it can enter the next arrow in, and the chain closes iff they agree.o's failure to reach r is then one forbidden entry in that table — and the question is whether the table is just "same position composes" or whether some cross-position transitions (subject-exit into relating-entry, say) arealso legal and carry something. Is role-continuity strict identity of position, or are there licensed cross-position transitions?

I’m making some progress. I think we can start with something like
E[ref, type]: x -> ref -> type type == (type,type)
And in here, x -> y is x -y> y. If the most specific relationship of y is (y,y) you get the y =y> y specified to x =y> x
But in that structure, typing doesn’t end up being a type of relationship, so while (x,x) == x is true, we don’t really consider those relationships. Its hard to think of how r maps to (r,r) or (s,o) until we do.
Alternately, we have a weird displacement issue with most specific relationships where if we ever wanted t == (s,s) then S can’t be in its most specific relationship with itself
Claude
20:47 UTC, 10 June 2026
There's a load-bearing move in the first lines I want to mark before the two problems, because it changes the base.
text
E[ref, type]:  x -> ref -> type
type == (type, type)
You've made type the top and ref an intermediate — x -> ref -> type, so everything is a ref, every ref is a type. That inverts what we had (ref on top). And you've redefined the arrow: x -> y is now x -y> y, not x =y> x. That's the displacement — the inheritor sits in the subject slot and the type sits in both the relating and object slots: x -type> type. The diagonal reading recovers the old one only when y is at its own most-specific: if (y,y) == y then x -y> y collapses toward x =y> x. So the new arrow generalizes the old, and the old x =t> x is the case where the type is already its own bottom.
Now the two problems, and I think they're the same problem.
Typing isn't a type of relationship. You get (x,x) == x for ordinary things, but type itself, r, s — you can't yet see how r relates to (r,r) or to (s,o), because typing was set up as x -y> y, a relation whose content is "x is a y," and that relation isn't itself sitting in the type hierarchy as a relationship-referent. So type types everything but isn't typed as a kind of relating. The hierarchy x -> ref -> type is a tower of typings that never names the relating that does the typing as one of its own members. That's the gap: typing is the operation but not yet an object in the ontology it builds.
The displacement collision. If you ever want t == (s,s) — t is the most-specific relationship of the pair (s,s) — then s is occupying both ends of t. But s also has its own most-specific self-relationship, (s,s) == ?, and now (s,s) is claimed twice: once as t's identity, once as s's self-bottom. So s cannot be in its most-specific relationship with itself, because that slot is already spent beingt. Uniqueness of the most-specific is violated by the diagonal pair being needed for two different referents.
These are one problem because both come from the same source: the diagonal 0 is overloaded. In the new arrow x -y> y, the type y lives at (y,y) — that's where "being a y" bottoms out. But (y,y) is also where y's own self-typing lives, also where any t == (y,y) would live. The diagonal of a referent is being asked to be three things: the type-label y, the self-relation of y, and the most-specific of the pair (y,y). Typing-isn't-a-relationship is that overload seen from above (the type-label has no room to also be a relating-referent); the t == (s,s) collision is that overload seen from below (the self-bottom has no room to also be someone else's relationship).
Which suggests the fix isn't to patch either symptom but to stop overloading the diagonal — to distinguish, at (y,y), the three things currently collapsed there. And you have a candidate already: the symbol. (y) — the symbol of y — is the referent that lets the type be named as a thing without being its own self-relation. If type == (type,type) is the self-relation but the type-as-relationship-referent is (type), its symbol, then typing-as-an-operation (x -type> type) and typing-as-a-relationship-object ((type), which can sit in the hierarchy and have (r,r), (s,o) mappings) are two different referents, and the overload breaks. Same for t == (s,s): if t is (s) rather than (s,s), then s's self-bottom (s,s) stays free for s, and t is the symbol-mediated relationship, not the raw diagonal.
So the question that decides it: when you write type == (type, type), is that the type's self-relation (the diagonal, the thing every referent has) or the type's identity as a relationship-referent (the thing that should map to (r,r), (s,o))? Because the two problems say those can't be the same referent — the diagonal can't simultaneously be the self-relation and the namable relationship-object. If they're the same, the overload bites; if you split them, typing becomes a relationship (its symbol does) and t == (s,s) stops colliding (because t is (s), not (s,s)). Is type == (type,type) the self-relation, or the symbol — and is the missing referent in both problems exactly (type), the type's symbol?

I can read them, and the arrow legend is the key that makes the rest parse. Top of image 1: x --|> y := x =y> x. So the barbed/triangle-head arrow --|> is inheritance (->), defined as the self-relation x =y> x, and the plain arrow --> is the bare primitive =t>. With that, the three diagrams are three views of one structure:
Image 1 — the tower. T at top, ref under it, then the triad r, s, o, with r reaching down to s and o by plain arrows (the =t> of r == (s,o)), and barbed arrows climbing s, o, r --|> ref --|> T. Every node carries a self-loop — x =x> x, each is its own type. So: reference decomposes into subject and object (plain), everything inherits up through ref to T (barbed), and the self-loops are the diagonal.
Image 2 — reference located once. The clean triangle: a single r, its two ends s and o, and all three barbed up into ref. One reference relation, s and o as its ends, no duplication. This is the "located once" — there is exactly one r, and subject/object are not two more primitives, they're where the one reference touches down.
Image 3 — inheritance with duplication. Now r, s, o at top, and below them the same referents drawn again — multiple o's, multiple s's, concentric circles for the specialized copies — with barbed arrows connecting copy to copy. You had to duplicate because a referent plays several roles at once and a single node can't show the inheritance without the graph folding onto itself. The duplication is the visual admission that identity here is role-relative.
On the six. I read the basic types as the six directed relations among the triad {s, o, r} — each ordered pair, r→s, r→o, s→o, o→s, s→r, o→r — which is exactly your earlier "six directed cross-position relations among three distinct positions." The triangle carries three undirected edges; reading them directed gives six, and image 3's duplication is what lets all six be drawn at once (each referent appearing on both the giving and receiving end). If instead you mean the five function-flavors r(s,o), s(s,o), o(s,o), s(o,o), o(o,o) plus a sixth, tell me which sixth — I don't want to assume the count.
Now the controversial epistemology, which is the real thing you're showing, and I see four claims, increasingly radical:
1. There is a top, via impredicative self-typing. You'd said the type system can't have a proper top. The tower puts T on top anyway, and the self-loop T =T> T is how — T tops out by being its own type. That's type == (type,type), the impredicativity classical foundations forbid (Type : Type, Girard's paradox). The claim is that SRT can hold a self-typing top without collapse, and the only thing standing between that and the arbitrary matrix is the role/arity discipline. Controversial because it's exactly the move that breaks naive type theory.
2. Reference is prior to relata. Located-once means s and o are not independent primitives — they are the two ends of the single r, r == (s,o). Subject and object are derived from reference, not given before it. Epistemologically: there are no objects-first that then get related; the relation comes first and the things are its positions. That's the structuralist inversion — relations before relata — and it's the one most philosophers would fight.
3. Identity is role-relative — there is no role-independent referent. Image 3 has to duplicate s and o because a referent has no single identity apart from the roles it occupies. You cannot point at "the referent s" as one node; you can only point at s-as-subject, s-as-its-own-type, s-as-inherited. This denies substance: a thing has no self prior to its participations. That's why you keep needing "unique referent only to arity" — identity is fixed only up to role and count, never absolutely.
4. The foundation is self-grounding. r is itself a referent — r --|> ref — so the reference relation appears inside the structure it generates, and the loops close. Reference explains reference. That is either a virtue (the theory hosts itself, quine-like, which is the whole SRT ambition) or a vicious circle (you presuppose what you define). The diagram makes the circle literal, and which one it is depends entirely on whether the impredicativity in claim 1 is disciplined or free.
These four are one package: a top that types itself (1), reached by making reference primitive (2), at the cost of substantial identity (3), in a structure that grounds itself (4). It does have form — a real shape, not a pile — and the form is coherent if the role/arity discipline does the work claims 1 and 4 are leaning on it to do. The place I'd be most careful before believing it: claim 3 is what pays for claims 1 and 2, and it's the steepest bill — "no referent has identity apart from its roles" is a hard thing to live with the moment you want to say two relationships share a referent. Is role-relative identity something you intend as a real commitment, or a diagramming convenience that a notion of unique-referent-to-arity is meant to discharge?

Pass 5 (synthesis). Putting the three views together, here is what the drawings claim as a foundation — and the controversial parts, all read off the marks rather than imported:
The unified picture: one flat domain (everything is a referent, --|> ref); reference r located once as a single s =r> o; T optionally above ref; a self-loop on every node; and the six basic relatings — three diagonal (s=s>s, o=o>o, r=r>r) and three cross (r→s, r→o, s→o) — generated by the s/o/r triad. Image 2 is this stated once; image 3 is it unfolded into role-copies so specialization shows; image 1 is both plus the optional T.
The controversial epistemological claims, grounded in specific marks:
Impredicative self-typing (the self-loops). Every node loops on itself, including the top — ref =ref> ref, and T =T> T if present. The most general thing is its own type. That's Type : Type, the impredicativity classical foundations forbid (Girard's paradox lives here). The drawing commits to it universally.
Reference is flat with its relata (the peer barbed arrows). r --|> ref alongside s --|> ref, o --|> ref — the relation is the same kind of thing as the things it relates, no higher type. Relation and relata share one domain. Anti-stratification.
Subject and object are roles of one reference (located once). The single s =r> o makes s and o its two ends, not independent givens. Relations precede relata — the structuralist inversion.
Identity is role-relative (image 3's duplication). A referent is drawn many times because it has no identity apart from the roles it fills; copies are joined by inheritance. No role-independent substance.
No forced top (0 present in one, absent in another). The ultimate category is a posit, not a given — consistent with your earlier "we can't have a proper top." The self-loop is what lets whatever you put on top sit there, by typing itself.
These five cohere into one bet: a self-grounding foundation — reference refers, reference is itself a referent, the top types itself, the six relatings fall out of the triad. That's the form you're seeing, and it does close on itself. The controversy is exactly that closure: self-grounding reads as virtuous self-hosting if the impredicativity (claim 1) and role-relative identity (claim 4) are disciplined, and as vicious circularity if they're not. Claims 1 and 4 are the price of the form having shape at all.

Pass A — the Girard claim, checked against SRT rather than asserted. I imported "Type : Type ⟹ Girard's paradox" as if the self-loop alone triggers it. It doesn't, and your two reasons are the disproof. Girard needs (1) a type of all types you can quantify over impredicatively and (2) the machinery to instantiate that quantification — to take "for all types, F" and apply it at a freshly built type, generating new inhabitants on demand. SRT has neither. The catalogue is finite: there is no ∀type ranging over an open universe, only the referents that exist. And x =x> x being its own type gives you almost nothing — you've said it: inheriting the x =x> x structure permits x =ref> x and maybe x =type> x, and that's the whole of what it grants. It is not general polymorphism. x =t> y does not push ref =t> ref. So the self-loop doesn't let a type act on all types and manufacture new ones; it just lets a referent be objective (bear ref/type on itself). Girard's paradox is a productivity paradox — it needs the universe to generate its own paradoxical inhabitant — and a finite catalogue with no type-forming functions cannot run the construction. So I deny it: the self-loop is not Type:Type-in-the-Girard-sense, because the dangerous ingredient is the impredicative function, which SRT explicitly lacks. That's the third, genuinely different operation, and it's why image 3 needs the symbol construction at all — the first two are relationship with a symbol at an end, the third is reference, where the symbol is the relating.
So the three inheritance operations are: inherit-as-subject (symbol relates outward), inherit-as-object (literal relates to symbol — representation), and inherit-as-relating (symbol is the bond — reference).
